## Forsaken shell

Forsaken shells were a type of undead consisting of a full set of skin that had been removed from the body of a slain or still-living creature. These undead could arise from almost any type of living creature, so long as it had skin, even dragons or giants.

While sometimes created by necromancers, they could otherwise arise spontaneously from a corpse with the influence of necrotic energy or be created by the deliberate workings of another forsaken shell.

Description
These undead were best described as being rather long and slender in form. They moved in a coiling and slithering fashion, much like a serpent. Most common varieties weighed in at around .

Personality
These undead exhibited an average level of intellect. Forsaken shells were aggressive beings that felt kinship towards one another and would seek to carry out vengeance upon anyone that dared to harm others of their kind, especially those that had been spawned by them.

All instances of this undead had a predatory nature to them, driven to attack the living by a need to propagate more of their kind, though they also were known to simply enjoy attacking living creatures.

Abilities
These undead could see in areas of total darkness. They exhibited an incredible degree of elasticity, strength, and flexibility with their bodies. This allowed them to easily escape most restraints and hide themselves in confined spaces, by folding up their bodies.

Prior to the Spellplague, forsaken shells would not retain any sort of abilities from the creatures they were spawned from. Following the Spellplague, it was not uncommon for forsaken shells to exhibit abilities that they had in life; such was the case with dragon shells.

The most unique trait of these undead was their ability to propagate more of themselves. This was done by stripping off skin from a slain living creature that possessed skin. Prior to the Spellplague, this skin would spontaneously transform into new forsaken shells that were under the command of their creator. Following the Spellplague, forsaken shells instead assimilated this skin into themselves and would then excrete from their bodies a new forsaken shell. Given time, a forsaken shell could potentially spawn thousands of its kind.

Combat
Most forsaken shells were ambush predators, preferring to rely on stealth and speed to overwhelm lone opponents. They fought by tightly constricting themselves around other creatures. Following the Spellplague, they were noted as being able to emit a harmful surge of necrotic energy by slapping themselves at a living creature.

They were generally not fearless beings who'd fight regardless of the odds. Rather, most would retreat when met with an overwhelming number of opponents, but those that arose from the skin of dragons were particularly brave and relied upon strength over speed. However, even the more common varieties of forsaken shell could be driven to reckless fighting if it meant avenging injuries that had been done to their kind. Titan shells relied primarily on strength over anything else, neither focusing their rage upon one opponent at a time nor strategizing.

After successfully slaying a living creature, forsaken shells would proceed to attempt to hide the body of their victim. They would usually place them in secluded areas, such as unfrequented closets or under piles of unused stuff.

Variants
Dragon shell
 Forsaken shells that arose from the skin of dragons. They notably maintained the dragon breath that their body had in life and their bites were infused with the corresponding elemental energy, such as acid or fire if it arose from the body of respectively a black dragon or red dragon, for example. Out of all forsaken shells, they were particularly vulnerable to radiant energy.
Titan shell
 Forsaken shells that arose from the skin of giants.

Society
These undead were typically found either in groups with other forsaken shells, generally numbering around two to four, or in the company of other undead. These undead were usually those that had a penchant for eating flesh, such as carcass eaters, ghouls, and zombies. However, they could also be found around poltergeists.

They might be animated purposely by a necromancer or arose spontaneously as a result of necrotic energy.

Habitats
Beyond the Prime Material plane, these undead could be found in the Domains of Dread and the demiplane known as the Night Realm.

History
Around the, a group of adventurers traveling on the mercantile vessel Rosy Dawn from Veltalar to the city of Relkath's Foot by means of the sea route called the Greenborn River were ambushed by a group of undead that included a dragon shell made from the skin of a black dragon, two drowned ghosts, and four sea wraiths.

Around the same century, a group of adventurers traveled through a portal beneath the city of Myth Drannor, taking them into Shazryl's tower in the Shadowfell. There they encountered a great deal of creatures under the alhoon's command, including a trio of forsaken shells that guarded a room alongside a tombstone golem.

Appendix
See Also
 Boneless, a similar sort of undead that were almost exclusive to the Domains of Dread.

## Forest runner

Forest runners were a variety of rangers that selflessly dedicated their lives to vigilantism, compelled by a personal code to fight against unjust or tyrannical systems of governance and to provide for the less fortunate that suffered under them.

Culture
While the title "forest runner" implied these rangers were restricted to woodlands, the "forest" part of the name could actually be swapped out with whatever terrain it was that they did business in, resulting in such alternate titles as desert runner or mountain runner for example.

Viewed by the powers they fought against as being no better than lawless rogues, these types of rangers always lived on the outskirts of society, making do with very little. Such rangers tended towards delighting in the torment of authority figures in the corrupt systems they fought against. They often found themselves viewed by the commoners as a sort of hero, which gave way to offerings of food and shelter.

Forest runners were often found living as loners due to their circumstances, constantly being on the run from the authorities. They were more than willing to join an adventuring company so as to escape from the law, spread justice further afield, or to acquire treasure for distribution to those less fortunate.

Abilities
All forest runners were skilled in the arts of making and fighting with bows. They were generally considered to be quite adept combatants. Much like rogues they typically had some skills in stealth and disguising themselves. And due to their charismatic nature, they could often invoke a rousing speech amongst their allies.

Just as other types of rangers honed their combat skills against particular foes, forest runners developed theirs in relation to the corrupt government they fought against. This creature could either be one that was against ones that was a symbol of the government, a pet of the tyrannical ruler, a creature that they wielded as a weapon of their authority, or one that was aligned with the corrupt government (such as an allied tribe of goblins).

Possessions
Typical weapons for a forest runner included such things as daggers, long bows, long swords, and quarterstaves.

Notable Forest Runners
 Myndhl, a forest runner that operated in the Dalelands in the late 14 century DR.
 Vaggit

## Fourwinds Shipping

Fourwinds Shipping was a shipping business in the Lower Port of Skullport in the late 14 century DR.

Structure
Fourwinds Shipping was a one-story building in fair condition.

Services
Approximately 50% of items shipped through Fourwinds became lost between origin and destination, a problem routinely blamed on the overworked and underpaid staff.

Fourwinds Shipping's shipping rates were calculated based on weight, distance, item delicacy, any special handling requirements, and whether the cargo was contraband along the route or at its destination, though Fourwinds Shipping did not accept live cargo. Standard rates started at 1 copper piece per . Despite their inefficiencies, Fourwinds Shipping continued to operate, catering to clients willing to gamble on the uncertain arrival of their goods.

Inhabitants
Fourwinds Shipping was owned by Takkee, a human thief. Known for his minimal competence in legitimate shipping and even poorer skills in smuggling, Takkee's lack of expertise often resulted in shipments going astray, which he blamed on his staff.

## Forester's brush hook

The forester's brush hook was a magical brush hook found in Uthe Lodge during the mid-1480s DR.

Description
The brush hook had a long, straight handle and a hooked blade, similar to a kama.

Powers
Despite being a fairly mundane tool, the forester's brush hook was enchanted to adaptively change its material and even alignment in order to bypass defenses and target weaknesses. For example, this allowed it to become silver to a werewolf, or cold iron to a fey, or adopt good alignment against a demon. On top of that, it was simply extremely sharp and deadly.

History
The blade was recovered from Uthe Lodge, home of the ranger Peron Uthe after he was turned into a red musk zombie by Halsaime.

## Fortress Narder

Fortress Narder was a ruined stone fort located in the far eastern edge of the Forest of Lethyr in the Great Dale.

History
It was built in antiquity by the Nar people under the Nentyarch Ulthas "the Darkwing" as a gathering point for the Nar armies debarking for Raumathar. It was destroyed during the last great conflict with Raumathar by some unidentified spell that reduced it to rubble.

In the  a foul taint seeped out of the ruined fortress, poisoning the water and soil in the land around, and foul creatures crept out from the ruins, hunting and slaying the wildlife nearby. In the, the leader of the Circle of Leth, the self-proclaimed Nentyarch, feared that the poisoning was due to a demoncyst located somewhere beneath the ruins, as hezrou demons were encountered lurking in the depths of the ancient fortress.

## Folklore of Faerûn

Folklore of Faerûn was a book that collected stories, legends, and folktales of the landlubber folk of Faerûn, published sometime before 1372 DR.

Contents
The tome compiled traditional folk prose and ballads of Faerûn. One of the stories of note was titled Sariah and the Ocean Strider. The story was about the titular Sariah Badgeriver, who once met an ocean strider during one of her daily fishing outings. Unbeknown to her, the ocean strider claimed the river as his kingdom, despite Sariah's family living on its banks for many generations. A spirited young woman, Sariah was not about to give up her family home to a "ravenous, gargantuan fey."

From there, the story branched into a number of variations, each described a different resolution of the conflict: one told a story of Sariah battling the strider to the death, while others told of how she tricked the wannabe monarch into relinquishing his claims over the river. Yet another version described a long journey Sariah Badgeriver embarked on on the ocean strider's behest in exchange for him leaving the river forever.

It was unknown where the tale was truthful or which of the version was the original tale, however, all of them had one thing in common: when the story was done, Sariah ended up up possessing the ocean strider's magic scimitar, be it through trickery or as an honest boon.

## Force screen

Force screen was a psionic power of the psychokinesis discipline that granted its user a protective shield of pure force.

Effects
When manifested, this created a invisible disk of force that followed the manifester, always hovering in front of them. This provided the manfiester creator protection from attacks, including incorporeal ones due to it being force-based power. The power lasted for a duration of one minute, extended by one or more additional minutes depending upon how skilled the psionics user was.

Displays
When used this power manifested an auditory display.

Users
This power was knowable by psions, psychic warriors, and wilders, as well as ardents and divine minds who followed the force mantle.

Force screen was innately possessed by thoon hulks, who had a force screen around their bodies at all times.

 Sysalok, a yuan-ti abomination psychic warrior.

## Force mantle

The Force mantle was a psionic mantle that granted ardents and divine minds an array of psionics powers and abilities derived from the universal concept of force energy.

Deities

Abilities
Those who followed the force mantle slightly took on the deflective qualities of force energy while psionically focused.

The psychic aura exuded by divine minds of the force mantle required the expenditure of a psionic focus to activate. Once done, the manifester and all allies within the radius of the aura became capable of striking at incorporeal creatures.

Powers
All of the powers granted by this mantle were of the psychokinesis discipline. Novice manifesters were granted deflection field, force screen, and concussion blast. Manifesters of some experience gained ethereal volley and telekinetic force. As a manifester progressed further they gained concussive detonation and telekinetic maneuver. Masters of this mantle obtained power psionic telekinetic sphere, allowing them to create and entrap other creatures within large mobile globes of force energy.

## Folding boat

A folding boat was a type of boat coming out of a small wooden box and became a fully fledged sailing boat.

Description
The boats always came in wooden boxes about  long by  wide and  deep, that acted just like normal boxes, but radiated magic.

Powers
Upon receiving their command word the box would unfold into a boat,  long by  wide and  deep, that acted boat with one pair of oars, an anchor, a mast, and lanteen sail. This little boat could carry three to four people. Upon another use of the command word and the boat would fold itself back into its box.Some versions of these boxes, would upon receiving the command word a second time instead fold out to an even bigger boat,  long by  wide and  deep, that was decked, had five sets of oars, a steering oar, an anchor, a deck cabin, a mast, and a square sail. This bigger boat could carry about 15 people. With a third use of the command word, this bigger boat would return into a wooden box.

History
An unknown adventurer, lost and dead in the mines of the Dungeon of Death, had carried one of these items, and there it still lay in  as the situation in those lost dwarven mines culminated.

## Foundation

The Foundation was a variety of enchanted light flails created on the Sword Coast North in the late 14 century DR, with the original and the most powerful variety once being wielded by the High Inquisitor of Auril.

Powers
There existed three variants of the Foundation, each possessed a +1, +4, and +8 enchantments what made the flail's bludgeoning damage deadlier and guided the wielder's hand towards the target. All three versions were ensorcelled with the knock spell of various strength, allowing the weapon to unlock, unbuckle, and unholster objects upon a strike.

The Foundation could be created from an enchanted light flail and a slab of ironwood.

History
The first Foundation was the weapon wielded by the High Inquisitor of Auril long before the late 14 century DR. She was a coldblooded and cruel woman who felt pleasure when she cracked the skulls of her enemies. At first, the satisfied this profane fetish my using an enchanted mace but as her desire grew, so did her need for a stronger weapon. When the Inquisitor got her hands on the Foundation she led her followers to attack a tribe of ogres who refused to join her Aurilite army. The Foundation killed many ogres but it disappeared alongside its mistress when the High Inquisitor followed an oni mage into the cave system that the ogres called home.

More common versions of the Foundation with a simpler +1 enchantment were forged by Master Blacksmith Marrok of Shining Knight Arms and Armor shop of Neverwinter to help the heroes who sought the cure from the dreaded Wailing Death of the . Later the same year, more powerful versions were created by Master Blacksmith Barun of Beorunna's Well to help the forces of Neverwinter stand against Lady Aribeth the Betrayer, who led a marching army of Luskan in the deadly but short-lived Neverwinter-Luskan war.

## Forest trapper

Forest trappers, also called miners, were a sub-species of trappers who had adapted to life in the woodlands of Faerûn.

Description
They quite resembled tailless manta rays, around  in diameter, with mottled or greenish brown skin adapted to better blend in with the woodlands they inhabited. Their frontal and dorsal ridges were hard bone. They had between 6 and 25 venomous spinal barbs running down their backs, which they used to hunt. At the base of each spine was a toxin sac where they stored their paralyzing venom.

Behavior
Forest trappers would travel quite slowly when on the move, either rippling like snakes on the surface or burrowing through soil, sand, and gravel. They tried to stay below the surface whenever possible for protection against enemies.

Combat
When hunting for prey, the forest trappers would burrow down a few inches into the ground, raise their spinal barbs up through the surface and letting the prey walk over them, hopefully hitting one or more spinal barbs and being injected with the forest trapper's venom. The venom would paralyze the prey. Only once all surface vibrations had seized would the forest trappers reveal themselves, dig out and envelop the paralyzed prey to feast.

Ecology
Forest trappers primarily preyed on small woodland animals, but were quite open to dining on more substantial meals, like that which traveled on roads.

They would lay their eggs in clutches several feet below the surface, and the young forest trappers would spent the first year of their life hunting and feeding on various burrowing creatures that they hunted without ever surfacing.

Habitation
They were most often encountered in the great Cormanthor forest,
but were found throughout the Eastern Heartlands in forested places.

Usage
The venom-filled spines of the forest trappers were sometimes used as ammunition in blowpipes, or as hand-weapons by small creatures who would wrap the grip as to not poison themselves.

## Forgotten Realms: Heroes of Faerûn

Forgotten Realms: Heroes of Faerûn is a campaign sourcebook for the Forgotten Realms using the 5-edition (revised)  ruleset published by Wizards of the Coast. It is set to be released in November 2025 and, alongside Forgotten Realms: Adventures in Faerûn, will be the first campaign setting book released for the Forgotten Realms in 5 edition, a full decade after Sword Coast Adventurer's Guide. Focusing on player options, it includes new subclasses, feats, backgrounds, and spells, as well as details on membership of heroic and villainous organizations.

Contents
The book includes eight new subclasses, eighteen player-character backgrounds, forty-two deities, thirty-four feats, eight factions, nineteen spells, three magic items, twelve mundane items, two monsters, and thirteen maps, as well as overviews of ten regions of Faerûn.

## Forgotten Realms: Adventures in Faerûn

Forgotten Realms: Adventures in Faerûn is a campaign sourcebook for the Forgotten Realms using the 5-edition (revised)  ruleset published by Wizards of the Coast. It is set to be released in November 2025 and, alongside Forgotten Realms: Heroes of Faerûn, will be the first campaign setting book released for the Forgotten Realms in 5 edition, a full decade after Sword Coast Adventurer's Guide. Oriented toward dungeon masters, it presents adventure ideas, monsters, and magic items, and an introductory adventure, "The Lost Library of Lethchauntos".

Contents
It includes five detailed gazetteers on Baldur's Gate (described as dark urban fantasy),  Calimshan, Dalelands, Moonshae Isles, and Icewind Dale (described as survival horror), with over fifty ready-to-run adventures, in addition to "The Lost Library of Lethchauntos". It also includes fifty-two maps, thirty-seven or forty monsters and villains, and seven or twelve magic items. It also includes rules for Bastions, allowing player-built strongholds.

## Folk hero

A folk hero, or peasant hero, was a common archetype of adventurer. Hailing from humble beginnings as a mere commoner, they invariably experienced some defining event that spurred them on the path of heroics by fighting against monsters and injustice, making a name for themselves, and becoming the pride of their village.

Culture
Whilst some felt the term specifically applied to fighters, adventurers of nearly any ilk could be folk heroes, from experts, to virtuous paladins, noble priests, mighty wizards, and master thiefs. In the 14 century DR, folk heroes were most commonly humans, followed by half-elves of moon or wild descent, forest gnomes, rock gnomes, and halflings. This was because demihumans weren't commonly part of the peasantry in human-dominated realms. When they were, they most often were living the life of a small-time farmer or herder. For example, many of the half-wild elves in Deepingdale and near the Yuirwood had human attitudes and farming lifestyles. Fire genasi were also typical folk heroes, for it was often common practice among them to seek out ways of enhancing their reputation.

Folk heroes varied greatly in the forms of professions they had prior to making the decision to become an adventurer, ranging from such things as farming, shepherding, woodcutting, fishing, hunting, mining, gravedigging, and simply being a servant. They could also emerge among minor craftsmen, such as bakers, blacksmiths, brewers, carpenters, cobblers, leatherworkers, masons, tailors and seamstresses, and weavers. But most commonly they were "peasants", meaning lower-class citizens tied to the land. However, there were some cases of people born to high society becoming folk heroes, such as Wyll "Blade of the Frontiers" Ravengard, by wholeheartedly devoting themselves to the plights of the commonfolk, earning their trust and adoration through their gallant deeds.

A folk hero could vary greatly in terms of their alignment, though owing to the expectations of such a role, heroes were usually virtuous individuals. Fighting against oppressive tyrants and monsters, freeing slaves, sheltering escaped slaves, and garnering wealth so that they could afford to help build much-needed structures in their home community. And because of their reputation, folk heroes were often looked upon as patrons of the commonfolk, someone who their community sought out in times of need.

Most folk heroes looked back upon their humble origins as a sort of blessing in disguise and cherished the communities they grew up in. Their origins allowed them to often find fellow commoners that would offer them and their companions a place to rest or hide from anyone searching for them, such as the law or a tyrannical ruler's troops, though sometimes it would only be so long as they didn't put the commoner's life in danger. They could also find commoners willing to give them food and drink, clothing, healing, what little weaponry they could scrape together, and potentially even minor hirelings. Even formerly sour neighbors would often change their tune for a peasant who became a famous folk hero. And the legacy of their good deeds could carry on to their descendants, enhancing their reputation among the commonfolk.

However, the benefits of being a folk hero were conditional. If a hero strayed from their down-to-earth ways or mistreated their former friends, they could find themselves shut out from their community. Furthermore, if a folk hero did manage to strike it rich whilst adventuring, they were likely to find themselves pestered from friends, neighbors, and any local beggars to share in the wealth. Some might resort to pressing charges against the hero for true or false misdeeds of the past, whilst others would make pleas of their blood relation in order to get some sort of gift or loan.

In several countries in Faerûn, with Tethyr being one such example, peasants had to swear an oath of loyalty to their liege. Because of this, a peasant hero could find themselves being rewarded by their liege for their bravery and then commanded to join the military or take on some quest or other responsibility. Refusal to obey their liege could incur an arrest or other troubles.

Possessions
In the early stages of their career as an adventurer, the appearance of a folk hero was not too dissimilar from that of the average peasant. Their clothing would be often dirty, yet durable, with gloves and shoes supplemented by cloth wrappings. And being hard workers they were often unhygienic and displayed callused hands. They usually were not adorned in armor unless they were out adventuring, and when they were it was typically either hide or leather armor.

In the early stages of their career, a hero's weaponry was limited to whatever was most available to the peasantry. This often meant long and short bows, clubs, daggers, footman's flails, footman's picks, hand axes, knives, machetes, military forks, quarterstaves, short swords, spears, slings, staff slings, sickles, and warhammers. A good deal of these were derived from tools that the peasant hero used in their daily life, such as mattocks for digging, threshing flails for beating the grain, or pitchforks for moving hay.

Other tools of the peasantry that could find use as weapons in the hands of a folk hero ranged the gamut of billhooks, various types of hoes, hunting knives, rakes, reavers and scythes, shears, shepherd's crooks, shovels, fisherman's tridents, and weeding claws.

Combat
Unlike more experienced warriors, a peasant hero often lacked the know-how for special combat maneuvers or martial arts.

History
Over the course of Faerûnian history, there were many wars and disasters that inspired commoners to rise above their station and become folk heroes. These included Cormyr's stand against the Tuigan Horde, Amn's war with the Sythillisian Empire, the nation of Thay's intrigues against Aglarond and the Dalelands, and the Tethyrian Interregnum and Reclamation Wars. Even the occasional attacks from humanoids or giants upon the people of Luiren could provide the spark necessary to become a folk hero.

In the late 15 century, the merchant Syndra Silvane became afflicted with a condition called the death curse. After clerics were unable to help her, she contacted the Harpers, who indicated to her that the cause was a necromantic artifact in Chult called the Soulmonger. After learning this news, Syndra gathered information about Chult and began hiring teams of adventurers to travel into its jungle depths. At least a few of these adventurers she'd end up recruiting would be sought due to the reputation they had amassed as folk heroes.

Notable Folk Heroes
 The Blue Blade, a gallant brigand who became something of a folk hero in Cormyr for his robbing of rich travelers in King's Forest.
 Bromdurr Tathen, a peasant hero who gained fame after helping defend his hometown of Memnon from pirates.
 Danton, a tiefling druid from the Western Heartlands in the late 15 century DR.
 Gellana Mirrorshade, an affluent cleric of Garl Glittergold who was renowned as a folk hero by gnomes across the Western Heartlands.
 Hadarr Karayaka, a folk hero among the Calishite community of Daggerford.
 Halsaime, an elder druid of Thorngap who was considered something of a folk hero among Cormyrians before he was driven insane by a strange staff in the late 15 century DR.
 Herman Gresham, a blacksmith who became a peasant hero among the people of the small village of Gresham.
 Ichi the Highwayman, a rogue who was renowned as a folk hero among the people of Miyama in the 12 century DR.
 Minsc, a Rashemaar warrior that earned fame throughout the Sword Coast in the 14 and 15 centuries DR.
 Peredus the Great, a folk hero in Daggerford who was renowned for defending caravans.
 Ridal Sureblade, a peasant hero from the barony of Karondar who led his fellow commoners in overthrowing their tyrannical baron.
 Wyll Ravengard, a warlock adventurer who went by the moniker "Blade of the Frontiers" on the Sword Coast in the late 15 century DR.
 Zahara the Shark, a famous swashbuckler-thief folk hero and captain of a small privateer fleet on the Sword Coast called Zahara's Krakens.

## Fort Calim

Fort Calim, also known as "Shoonach's Sunset", was one of the main border forts of County Calimmon in Tethyr, together known as the Calimmon Garrisons.

Location
This fort was located in eastern Calimmon, north of the River Agis and very close to the border of County Monteshi, the ruins of Shoonach, and Fort Teqlar.

Structure
Like most of the Calimmon Garrisons, this fortress had a simple but effective stone construction. Its walls were ten-to-fifteen-feet thick (3.0–4.6 meters) and 20 feet high (6.1 meters) with a deep, empty trench around them.

Defenses
Fort Calim was defended by around 200 Tethyrian soldiers, overseen by 10 officers and the fort commander.

History
The Fort was built on the ancient foundations of a long-lost dwarven fortress, likely from the time of the First Kingdom of Mir around -5300 DR. As of the 14 century DR, the fort was approximately 600 years old. Due to its position close to Shoonach, it served equally as a bulwark against Calishite aggression and monsters emerging from the Shoonach ruins.

Appendix
Reference

Connections

---

## Fort Teqlar

Fort Teqlar was a Tethyrian military outpost established inside the ruins of Shoonach, former capital of the Shoon Imperium.

Geography
Fort Teqlar was on the northwestern edge of Shoonach in the Fedhiyajar, just inside the city's curtain wall west of Iltakar and the gate to Darromar. It counted as being inside County Monteshi and Duchy Ankaram.

Defenses
The fort was staffed by a garrison of 150 Tethyrian soldiers, and three patrols of 25 soldiers that ranged out into the ruins to destroy threats, one in the day and two at night.

History
The fort was restored from a ruined structure after the Tethyrian Interregnum, overseen by Count Oxsal Keeneyes of Monteshi. He named the fort after a comrade killed in the Storm Siege of Myratma, and effectively moved his court there from his actual home at Pelmarin. He saw his primary duty as maintaining the guard over the ruins of Shoonach, and preventing its dangers from emerging and threatening Tethyr, especially the nearby capital of Darromar.

## Fog rock

Fog rocks were small, naturally magical stones that were known to wash up on the western shores of islands in the Sea of Swords.

Description
These rocks apparently manifested in the Trackless Sea, and could be found along the western coasts of the Moonshae Isles and the Nelanther Isles. Sages theorized that they collected magic directly from the Weave via some unknown but natural process.

Powers
When dropped into fresh water, the stones produced a  cloud of thick mist, which then dispersed normally. They did not produce this effect in salt water, and they had to be kept damp and salty to maintain their magic.
